# Message replacement table for this CPython fork.
#
# One entry per line.  The two halves are separated by a TAB:
#
#     <key><TAB><hex-encoded UTF-8 replacement>
#     division by zero<TAB>e7aca8e89b8be6898de4bc9ae68a8a30e694bee588b0e999a4e695b0e590a721
#
# Blank lines and lines starting with '#' are ignored.
#
# The key is the message as it is, so it may hold commas, spaces, quotes and
# parentheses without any escaping.  A TAB is the separator and cannot appear
# in a key; neither can a newline, which ends the line.  Blanks around the
# separator are trimmed, so "key<TAB>hex", " key <TAB> hex" and
# "key<TAB>hex<TAB>" all read the same.  A line without a TAB, a line without a
# key and a line without a value are malformed and are skipped.
#
# The right-hand side is exactly what bytes.hex() produces, so generate it with
#
#     print(input().encode('utf-8').hex())
#
# Why hex and not the text itself: it keeps the whole file ASCII, so the file
# cannot be reinterpreted by whatever encoding the editor that saved it decided
# to use.  Saving this table as UTF-16 makes every line malformed, and saving it
# as UTF-8 *with a BOM* is worse -- the BOM becomes the first character of the
# key, so that one entry silently never matches while everything else looks
# fine.  ASCII has neither failure mode.  The cost is that a replacement takes
# twice the bytes it needs; at ~140 bytes per entry that is not worth trading
# for a class of silent failures.
#
# The table is read once, on first use, and stays in memory.  A missing or
# malformed table degrades silently: the original message is always printed.
#
# Lookup order (see Objects/message_table.c): $PYTHON_MESSAGE_TABLE, then
# $PYTHONHOME/lookup-table.txt, then ./lookup-table.txt in the current
# directory.  This file is the shipped table; copy it next to the installed
# interpreter, or point $PYTHON_MESSAGE_TABLE at it.
#
# Two kinds of key:
#
#   * no '*'  --  a literal message.  The text the exception displays has to be
#                 equal to it, and the replacement is used as it is.
#   * a '*'   --  a pattern.  '*' stands for any run of characters, possibly
#                 empty; the runs are numbered in order of appearance and the
#                 replacement refers to the n-th of them as "%n".
#
# Matching is non-greedy: every '*' takes the shortest run that still lets the
# rest of the pattern match.  A literal key always wins over a pattern, and
# among patterns the one with the fewest '*' wins (ties: the first in the
# file).  A replacement naming a capture the pattern does not have ("%2" under
# a key with one '*', or "%0") is refused and the original message is printed.
#
# Patterns are matched against the text the exception displays, so they reach
# OSError, UnicodeDecodeError, KeyError and types with their own __str__ too --
# the key has to be what str(exc) produces on that platform.
#
# The text a copy displays is not always the matched text: a type whose __str__
# ignores args (UnicodeDecodeError, a type with its own __str__) is matched but
# the replacement cannot reach its display, and an OSError carrying errno shows
# its whole args tuple.  Such a rewrite is dropped and the original is printed;
# Objects/exceptions.c has the details.
#
# Quotes are ordinary literal text.  In "'*'" both quotes belong to the pattern
# and are consumed by it, so the capture is `int` and not `'int'`, and a
# replacement that wants the quoted form has to spell the quotes out itself, as
# the entries below do.  Putting the '*' right up against the quotes
# ("* object has no attribute *") captures them instead, and then the
# replacement must not add them a second time.
#
# Messages to try first: keep these two working, FORK-NOTICE.md cites them.
division by zero	e7aca8e89b8be6898de4bc9ae68a8a30e694bee588b0e999a4e695b0e590a7efbc81

#
# More entries, same recipe.
